### 134. Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis

**PEARL:** A child with **SEBORRHOEIC-LIKE SCALP/FLEXURAL RASH + PUNCHED-OUT LYTIC BONE LESIONS (classically SKULL) + recurrent otitis media/hearing loss ± hepatosplenomegaly and lymphadenopathy**. Biopsy: **histiocytes with BIRBECK GRANULES (tennis-racquet shaped) on electron microscopy**, staining **CD1a, S-100 and CD207 (langerin) positive**.

**UNDERSTAND:** LCH is a **clonal proliferation of Langerhans-type dendritic cells** (most carry a **BRAF V600E** mutation) — so it behaves like a neoplasm but presents with what looks like eczema and ear infection. The bone lesions are **osteolytic because the histiocytes recruit osteoclasts**; skull and mastoid involvement explains the deafness.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **The classical eponyms:** **LETTERER–SIWE** — acute disseminated, in infants, with rash, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy and cytopenias (worst prognosis); **HAND–SCHÜLLER–CHRISTIAN** — the triad of **lytic skull lesions, EXOPHTHALMOS and DIABETES INSIPIDUS**; **EOSINOPHILIC GRANULOMA** — a solitary bone lesion in an older child (best prognosis).
- **DIABETES INSIPIDUS from pituitary stalk infiltration is the commonest permanent sequela** — always ask about polyuria and polydipsia.
- **A chronic discharging ear or “seborrhoeic dermatitis” that fails to respond to treatment in an infant should raise LCH** — the most useful clinical prompt.
- **Skull X-ray shows “punched-out” lytic lesions with BEVELLED EDGES** and, in the mandible, “**floating teeth**”.
- **BRAF V600E mutations** make **BRAF inhibitors** (vemurafenib, dabrafenib and encorafenib) effective in refractory disease; localised disease may need only curettage or observation.
- **Distinguish the histology options: lymphoblasts CD3+ → T-ALL; plasma cells with Russell bodies → chronic inflammation/myeloma; Reed–Sternberg cells → Hodgkin lymphoma.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Infant + seborrhoeic rash + lytic skull lesion + ear discharge → **Birbeck granules**.

**ALSO KNOW:** The rash is **scaly, crusted, petechial, in the scalp and flexures** — mistaken for cradle cap or nappy rash that “won’t clear”. Skeletal signs: punched-out skull lesions **WITHOUT a sclerotic rim**, a **BEVELLED EDGE / “hole-within-a-hole”**, **BUTTON SEQUESTRUM**, **FLOATING TEETH** (loss of the lamina dura) and **VERTEBRA PLANA** (LCH is the commonest cause in children). **DIABETES INSIPIDUS from pituitary stalk involvement is the commonest endocrine complication.**

**REMEMBER:** *Rash, punched-out skull, and a discharging ear — look for tennis racquets under the electron microscope.*

---

### 135. Multiple Myeloma — Bortezomib and Hypercalcaemia

**PEARL:** **PROTEASOME INHIBITORS (bortezomib, carfilzomib) kill myeloma cells by causing INCREASED APOPTOSIS** — accumulated misfolded protein produces ER stress, and **NF-κB signalling is inhibited**. In **severe HYPERCALCAEMIA, the FIRST step is aggressive IV NORMAL SALINE** — not chemotherapy, bisphosphonates or transplant.

**UNDERSTAND:** Plasma cells are **protein factories** producing vast quantities of immunoglobulin — so they are **uniquely dependent on the proteasome** to clear misfolded protein. Blocking it floods the cell with toxic protein aggregates. This is **selective toxicity by exploiting a cell’s own overproduction**, and it is why the drug class works in myeloma above all other cancers.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Hypercalcaemia sequence: (1) AGGRESSIVE IV SALINE (these patients are profoundly volume-depleted from calcium-induced nephrogenic DI) → (2) IV BISPHOSPHONATE (zoledronic acid) or DENOSUMAB → (3) CALCITONIN for rapid but transient effect → (4) treat the underlying myeloma.** **Loop diuretics are NOT routine** — only after volume repletion, for fluid overload.
- **CRAB features define symptomatic myeloma: hyperCalcaemia, Renal impairment, Anaemia, Bone lesions.** Plus the **SLiM criteria** (≥60% clonal plasma cells, involved:uninvolved free light chain ratio ≥100, >1 focal MRI lesion).
- **Imaging: LYTIC lesions with NO sclerotic rim and NO uptake on bone scan** (osteoblasts are suppressed) — hence a **skeletal survey or whole-body low-dose CT/PET-CT, not a bone scan**.
- **Renal failure in myeloma is multifactorial: cast nephropathy (light chains), hypercalcaemia, dehydration, hyperuricaemia, amyloidosis and NSAIDs.** **Avoid contrast and NSAIDs.**
- **Bortezomib’s key toxicity is PAINFUL PERIPHERAL NEUROPATHY** (reduced by subcutaneous administration) and **herpes zoster reactivation — give aciclovir prophylaxis**.
- **PRIMARY PLASMA CELL LEUKAEMIA** — **>20% circulating plasma cells (or >2000/µL) with NO prior myeloma** — is a distinct, aggressive entity; **secondary PCL** is leukaemic transformation of known myeloma.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most appropriate INITIAL management” of myeloma with calcium 14.8 → **IV normal saline**. And “effect of a proteasome blocker” → **increased apoptosis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Fluid first, then the bisphosphonate, then the myeloma. And the proteasome inhibitor works because plasma cells make too much protein.*

**IMAGES:**
![question](https://corebtr-assets-production.s3.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/questions_image/01KM50P4ZA4CXES17EXXQDMP9H.jpg)

---

### 136. Chronic Lymphocytic Leukaemia

**PEARL:** Elderly patient, **painless lymphadenopathy, absolute LYMPHOCYTOSIS, and SMUDGE (basket) CELLS** on the smear. **Confirm with FLOW CYTOMETRY OF PERIPHERAL BLOOD** — a marrow biopsy is not needed for diagnosis. Immunophenotype: **CD19, CD20 (dim), CD23 and CD5 POSITIVE with light-chain restriction**.

**UNDERSTAND:** CLL circulates, so **the diagnostic material is already in the peripheral blood** — flow cytometry demonstrating a **monoclonal B-cell population (light chain restricted)** proves clonality and defines the phenotype in one inexpensive test. Marrow, FISH and node biopsy answer different questions (staging, prognosis, transformation).

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Smudge/basket cells are fragile lymphocytes crushed during smear preparation** — a classic morphological clue, though not specific.
- **Diagnostic threshold: ≥5000/µL monoclonal B lymphocytes** in the blood. Below that with no nodes = monoclonal B lymphocytosis.
- **FISH is for PROGNOSIS: del(13q) is favourable; TRISOMY 12 intermediate; del(11q) and especially del(17p)/TP53 mutation are ADVERSE and predict chemo-resistance** — these patients get **BTK inhibitors (ibrutinib, acalabrutinib) or venetoclax (BCL2 inhibitor)** rather than chemoimmunotherapy.
- **Complications: AUTOIMMUNE HAEMOLYTIC ANAEMIA and ITP (paradoxical autoimmunity), HYPOGAMMAGLOBULINAEMIA with recurrent infection (the leading cause of death), and RICHTER TRANSFORMATION to diffuse large B-cell lymphoma** — suspect with **rapid nodal enlargement, B symptoms and a rising LDH → this is when you do a NODE BIOPSY**.
- **Staging: RAI and BINET.** **Early asymptomatic disease is NOT treated** — “watch and wait” is standard and treating early does not prolong life.
- **CD5+ B cells** — remember only CLL and mantle cell (separated by CD23).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most appropriate investigation to CONFIRM” → **flow cytometry of peripheral blood**.

**REMEMBER:** *Smudge cells and lymphocytosis in an older adult — flow the blood, don’t stick the marrow.*

---

### 137. Dry Tap on Bone Marrow Aspiration

**PEARL:** A **“dry tap” (failure to aspirate marrow)** occurs with **FIBROSIS, packed hypercellularity or dense infiltration** — classically **PRIMARY MYELOFIBROSIS, HAIRY CELL LEUKAEMIA, AML-M7 (acute megakaryoblastic), myelodysplastic syndrome (fibrotic variant), and metastatic marrow infiltration**. It **mandates a TREPHINE BIOPSY**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Aspiration only works if marrow cells are **free enough to be sucked out**. Anything that lays down **reticulin/collagen** — or packs the marrow so densely that it cannot flow — produces a dry tap. **Megakaryocytes are the fibrosis-driving cell** (they release PDGF and TGF-β), which is why both **myelofibrosis and AML-M7 (a megakaryoblastic leukaemia)** are on the list.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **HAIRY CELL LEUKAEMIA:** middle-aged man, **MASSIVE SPLENOMEGALY WITHOUT lymphadenopathy, PANCYTOPENIA with MONOCYTOPENIA (characteristic)**, and cells with fine cytoplasmic projections that are **TRAP-POSITIVE (tartrate-resistant acid phosphatase)** and **CD11c, CD25, CD103 positive**, with a **BRAF V600E** mutation. **Treatment: CLADRIBINE** (a purine analogue) — dramatically effective.
- **PRIMARY MYELOFIBROSIS:** **JAK2, CALR or MPL** mutation; **massive splenomegaly with EXTRAMEDULLARY HAEMATOPOIESIS**, and a **LEUCOERYTHROBLASTIC blood picture with TEARDROP CELLS (dacrocytes)**. Treat with **ruxolitinib**.
- **AML-M7** is strongly associated with **DOWN SYNDROME** in children.
- **Always send the trephine for reticulin staining** when a dry tap occurs — that is the diagnostic step.
- **Follicular lymphoma infiltrates the marrow (paratrabecular aggregates) but does NOT usually cause fibrosis** — hence it is the exception in the question.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Multi-select — **hairy cell, AML-M7 and MDS** are causes; **follicular lymphoma** is not.

**REMEMBER:** *If nothing comes out, something has filled it or fibrosed it — take the trephine.*

---

### 138. Chloroma (Myeloid Sarcoma)

**PEARL:** **Chloroma = granulocytic/myeloid sarcoma — an extramedullary tumour of immature myeloid cells**, classically causing **BILATERAL PROPTOSIS in a child**. It signifies **acute myeloid leukaemia**, so the **next investigation is BONE MARROW EXAMINATION**.

**UNDERSTAND:** A chloroma is **AML growing outside the marrow** — so finding one is not the end of the diagnostic process but the beginning: you must establish the **underlying systemic disease, the blast percentage, and the cytogenetics** that will determine treatment. Flow cytometry and molecular studies are then performed **on the marrow sample**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **The name “chloroma” comes from its GREEN colour** on cut section — due to **MYELOPEROXIDASE** in the immature myeloid cells (it fades on exposure to air).
- **Sites: orbit (hence proptosis), skin (“leukaemia cutis”), bone, lymph nodes, CNS and gums.** **GUM HYPERTROPHY (gingival infiltration) is characteristic of the MONOCYTIC subtypes AML-M4 and M5.**
- **It is strongly associated with t(8;21) AML**, and may precede marrow involvement by months — **isolated myeloid sarcoma must still be treated as systemic AML**.
- **Differential for childhood proptosis: NEUROBLASTOMA metastasis (with periorbital ecchymoses — “raccoon eyes”), rhabdomyosarcoma, LCH, orbital cellulitis, and retinoblastoma** (which causes leucocoria rather than proptosis early).
- **Treat with systemic AML chemotherapy** — local radiotherapy alone is inadequate.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Next investigation of choice” → **bone marrow examination** (flow cytometry is done *on* that sample, so it cannot be the next step).

**REMEMBER:** *Green tumour, myeloperoxidase, bilateral proptosis in a child — it is AML until the marrow proves otherwise.*

---

### 139. Sideroblastic Anaemia

**PEARL:** The iron profile is the **MIRROR IMAGE of iron deficiency: HIGH serum iron, HIGH transferrin SATURATION, HIGH ferritin, LOW/normal TIBC**, with a **DIMORPHIC blood picture** and **marked ERYTHROID HYPERPLASIA** with **RING SIDEROBLASTS** in the marrow.

**UNDERSTAND:** The defect is in **incorporating iron into haem, not in obtaining iron**. Iron therefore **arrives at the erythroblast normally but cannot be used**, and accumulates in the **perinuclear MITOCHONDRIA — producing the ring sideroblast** (demonstrated by **PERLS’ PRUSSIAN BLUE** stain). The marrow works harder (erythroid hyperplasia) but produces defective cells — **ineffective erythropoiesis**, which is why iron overload develops without transfusion.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **The DIMORPHIC picture (a population of hypochromic microcytes alongside normal/macrocytic cells) is the clue on the smear** — and is also seen in mixed deficiency and after transfusion.
- **Causes: HEREDITARY (X-linked, ALAS2 — often PYRIDOXINE-RESPONSIVE)** and **ACQUIRED — MYELODYSPLASTIC SYNDROME (MDS-RS, ==associated with SF3B1 mutations==), LEAD poisoning, ALCOHOL, ISONIAZID and other B6 antagonists, chloramphenicol, linezolid, COPPER DEFICIENCY (or zinc excess) and hypothermia.**
- **Always ask about isoniazid and alcohol** — the two commonest reversible causes; **give pyridoxine** in INH-associated and in hereditary forms.
- **Contrast the microcytic anaemias:** **iron deficiency — low iron, HIGH TIBC, low saturation, low ferritin, RAISED RDW**; **thalassaemia trait — normal iron studies with a very high red cell count and a LOW MENTZER INDEX (MCV/RBC <13)**; **anaemia of chronic disease — low iron, LOW TIBC, normal/high ferritin, raised hepcidin**; **sideroblastic — high iron and saturation**.
- **Treatment: remove the cause, pyridoxine, transfusion with IRON CHELATION** — and **never give iron**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Which would NOT be seen?” → **low serum iron** (it is high).

**REMEMBER:** *Plenty of iron that the cell cannot use — the exact opposite of iron deficiency, and never give more iron.*

---

### 140. Heavy Chain Diseases

**PEARL:** **ALPHA heavy chain disease is the COMMONEST** — also called **SELIGMANN DISEASE, Mediterranean lymphoma, or IMMUNOPROLIFERATIVE SMALL INTESTINAL DISEASE (IPSID)**, and it is a form of **MALT lymphoma of the small bowel**. **Gamma (FRANKLIN) disease** and **mu heavy chain disease** are rarer.

**UNDERSTAND:** In heavy chain disease the clone produces a **truncated immunoglobulin HEAVY chain with NO bound LIGHT chain** — the mirror image of light-chain (Bence Jones) myeloma. Because the alpha isotype is the **mucosal immunoglobulin (IgA)**, alpha HCD is a **disease of the gut mucosa** — which is why it presents with malabsorption rather than bone disease.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **IPSID presents in young adults in the Mediterranean, Middle East and South Asia with chronic DIARRHOEA, MALABSORPTION, weight loss, abdominal pain and clubbing** — associated with poor sanitation and with ***CAMPYLOBACTER JEJUNI*** infection.
- **EARLY-STAGE IPSID CAN RESPOND TO ANTIBIOTICS ALONE (tetracycline)** — the same principle as **gastric MALT lymphoma regressing after *H. pylori* eradication**. This antigen-driven, antibiotic-responsive behaviour is the most remarkable fact about the condition.
- **Gamma HCD (Franklin) presents like a lymphoma with lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly and characteristically PALATAL OEDEMA from Waldeyer’s ring involvement.**
- **Mu HCD is associated with CLL** and shows **vacuolated plasma cells** in the marrow.
- **Fit them into the plasma cell dyscrasias: MGUS → smouldering myeloma → multiple myeloma → plasma cell leukaemia; plus WALDENSTRÖM MACROGLOBULINAEMIA (IgM, lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma, HYPERVISCOSITY treated with plasmapheresis), AL amyloidosis, and the heavy chain diseases.**
- **Diagnosis is by serum/urine IMMUNOFIXATION showing a heavy chain WITHOUT a light chain.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most common heavy chain disease” → **alpha (Seligmann disease)**.

**REMEMBER:** *Alpha is the gut immunoglobulin — so alpha HCD is a small bowel lymphoma that can be cured with an antibiotic.*

---

### 141. Which Anaemias Respond to Erythropoietin

**PEARL:** EPO works where **endogenous erythropoietin is INAPPROPRIATELY LOW for the degree of anaemia** — principally **ANAEMIA OF CHRONIC KIDNEY DISEASE**, and also **MYELOFIBROSIS with a serum EPO <125 U/L**, anaemia of inflammation, and chemotherapy-induced anaemia. It does **not** work in **aplastic anaemia** or **nutritional anaemia**.

**UNDERSTAND:** EPO is a **growth factor, not a substrate**. It can only stimulate a marrow that **has the cells and the raw materials to respond**. In **aplastic anaemia there are no progenitors to stimulate**; in **nutritional anaemia the bricks (iron, B12, folate) are missing** — so the correct treatment is replacement, not stimulation.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **ALWAYS ENSURE IRON SUFFICIENCY BEFORE AND DURING EPO THERAPY** — EPO dramatically increases iron demand, and **functional iron deficiency is the commonest cause of EPO resistance**. IV iron is often needed in dialysis patients.
- **Target haemoglobin 10–11.5 g/dL — do NOT normalise it.** Trials (**CHOIR, CREATE, TREAT**) showed that targeting normal haemoglobin **increases stroke, thrombosis, hypertension and mortality**, and in cancer patients may promote tumour progression.
- **Adverse effects: HYPERTENSION (the commonest), thrombosis/vascular access clotting, seizures, and rarely PURE RED CELL APLASIA from anti-EPO antibodies.**
- **Other causes of EPO resistance: infection/inflammation, hyperparathyroidism, aluminium toxicity, blood loss, and malignancy.**
- **In CKD, anaemia is multifactorial: EPO deficiency (dominant), shortened red cell survival, uraemic inhibitors of erythropoiesis, blood loss, and iron deficiency.**
- **Aplastic anaemia treatment: immunosuppression (ATG + ciclosporin) or ALLOGENEIC STEM CELL TRANSPLANT** in the young with a matched donor; plus **eltrombopag**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Multi-select → **CKD anaemia and myelofibrotic anaemia** respond; aplastic and nutritional do not.

**REMEMBER:** *EPO is a whip, not a fuel — it needs a marrow that still has cells and iron.*

---

### 142. Haemophilia — Pattern of Bleeding

**PEARL:** The commonest bleeding manifestation of **severe haemophilia is RECURRENT HAEMARTHROSIS**, most often into the **KNEE** (“target joint”). Severity is graded by residual factor activity: **SEVERE <1%, MODERATE 1–5%, MILD 6–30%.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Coagulation factor deficiency causes **DEEP tissue and joint bleeding with DELAYED onset**, because the platelet plug forms normally but **cannot be stabilised by fibrin** — so bleeding stops and then restarts hours later. **Platelet and vessel disorders instead cause IMMEDIATE, SUPERFICIAL mucocutaneous bleeding** — petechiae, purpura, epistaxis and menorrhagia. That single contrast answers most bleeding-disorder questions.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **X-LINKED RECESSIVE: HAEMOPHILIA A = FACTOR VIII (commoner), HAEMOPHILIA B (Christmas disease) = FACTOR IX. They are clinically INDISTINGUISHABLE — only the factor assay separates them.**
- **Laboratory: PROLONGED aPTT with NORMAL PT, NORMAL platelet count and NORMAL bleeding time.** A **MIXING STUDY CORRECTS** in factor deficiency but **fails to correct with an INHIBITOR** (acquired factor VIII antibodies or lupus anticoagulant).
- **Blood in a joint is chemically destructive: iron-laden synovium → SYNOVITIS → cartilage destruction → CHRONIC HAEMOPHILIC ARTHROPATHY and fixed deformity.** Hence prophylaxis, not on-demand treatment, is the modern standard.
- **Treatment: recombinant factor concentrate; DESMOPRESSIN (DDAVP) works in MILD haemophilia A and von Willebrand disease (releases stored VIII/vWF) but NOT in haemophilia B; tranexamic acid for mucosal bleeds; EMICIZUMAB (a bispecific antibody bridging IXa and X) has transformed prophylaxis, including for those with inhibitors.**
- **INHIBITOR DEVELOPMENT is the major complication of therapy** — suspect it when bleeding stops responding to factor.
- **AVOID intramuscular injections, aspirin and NSAIDs.** **Muscle haematomas can cause COMPARTMENT SYNDROME**; a retroperitoneal/iliopsoas bleed mimics appendicitis and causes femoral nerve palsy.
- **VON WILLEBRAND DISEASE is the commonest INHERITED bleeding disorder overall** (autosomal dominant) — mucocutaneous bleeding, prolonged bleeding time, abnormal ristocetin aggregation, and a mildly prolonged aPTT.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most common bleeding manifestation of severe haemophilia” → **recurrent haemarthrosis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Factors bleed deep and late into joints; platelets bleed shallow and at once into skin.*

---

### 143. Multiple Myeloma — Treat the Emergency First

**PEARL:** In myeloma with **hypercalcaemia (12 mg/dL), acute kidney injury (creatinine 6, urine output 50 mL/24 h) and severe anaemia**, the immediate step is **AGGRESSIVE IV FLUIDS + DEXAMETHASONE** — not definitive chemotherapy, not transplant, and not reflex dialysis. **HYPERCALCAEMIA is the commonest cause of renal failure in myeloma**, and it is reversible.

**UNDERSTAND:** Hypercalcaemia causes **nephrogenic diabetes insipidus and profound volume depletion**, which then causes **pre-renal failure and concentrates the light chains that form casts** — a self-amplifying loop. **Rehydration breaks the loop at both ends**: it restores perfusion and dilutes the nephrotoxic light chains. **Dexamethasone is both anti-myeloma and anti-hypercalcaemic**, and works within days — so it is the one drug that treats the emergency and the disease simultaneously.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Diagnostic criteria: ≥10% clonal marrow plasma cells (or a plasmacytoma) PLUS at least one myeloma-defining event — the “CRAB” features: hyperCalcaemia >11, Renal insufficiency (creatinine >2 or eGFR <40), Anaemia (Hb <10), Bone lytic lesions.** **≥60% plasma cells, a light-chain ratio ≥100, or >1 focal MRI lesion are now myeloma-defining even WITHOUT CRAB** (the “SLiM” criteria) — as in this patient.
- **Bone disease is PURELY LYTIC — “PUNCHED-OUT” lesions and the “RAIN-DROP/PEPPER-POT” skull — with a NORMAL ALKALINE PHOSPHATASE and a NEGATIVE bone scan**, because osteoblasts are suppressed. **Use skeletal survey, whole-body low-dose CT or PET-CT, not a radionuclide bone scan.**
- **Renal failure in myeloma is multifactorial: cast nephropathy (light chains + Tamm–Horsfall protein = “myeloma kidney”), hypercalcaemia, dehydration, hyperuricaemia, amyloidosis, and NSAIDs/contrast.** **Routine urine dipstick MISSES Bence Jones protein** (it detects albumin only) — you need **urine electrophoresis/immunofixation or serum free light chains**.
- **Other classic findings: ROULEAUX formation, a very high ESR, a NARROW ANION GAP (cationic paraprotein), an M-spike on electrophoresis with immune paresis, and recurrent ENCAPSULATED-organism infections — infection is the commonest cause of death.**
- **Hypercalcaemia treatment ladder: SALINE first, then ZOLEDRONIC ACID/pamidronate (works in 2–4 days; reduce dose in renal failure), CALCITONIN for rapid short-term effect, steroids in myeloma/lymphoma, and denosumab where bisphosphonates are contraindicated. AVOID loop diuretics until volume-replete, and avoid thiazides entirely.**
- **Definitive therapy: a triplet or quadruplet (bortezomib + lenalidomide + dexamethasone ± daratumumab) followed by AUTOLOGOUS stem cell transplant in fit patients** — but only after the metabolic emergency is corrected.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Next appropriate step” in myeloma with AKI and hypercalcaemia → **fluids + dexamethasone**.

**REMEMBER:** *Rehydrate before you treat the plasma cells — the calcium is what is killing the kidney.*

**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/56f896aa6c2b4c449e446165261ce012x458x918.PNG)

---

### 144. Vitamin B12: The Two Enzymes

**PEARL:** Vitamin B12 is a cofactor for exactly **two** human enzymes: **methionine synthase** (homocysteine + methyl-THF → methionine + THF) and **methylmalonyl-CoA mutase** (methylmalonyl-CoA → succinyl-CoA).

**UNDERSTAND:** Every clinical and biochemical feature of B12 deficiency falls out of these two reactions. Blocking **methionine synthase** traps folate as **methyl-THF (the “methyl-folate trap”)**, starving DNA synthesis of THF → **megaloblastic anaemia**, and raises **homocysteine**. Blocking the **mutase** causes accumulation of **methylmalonic acid**, which disrupts myelin synthesis → **subacute combined degeneration**. Folate can rescue the anaemia but not the mutase — which is why **giving folate alone to a B12-deficient patient corrects the blood picture while the neurology progresses**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Homocysteine raised in BOTH B12 and folate deficiency; methylmalonic acid raised ONLY in B12 deficiency** — MMA is the discriminating test.
- Blood picture: **MCV >100 fL, hypersegmented neutrophils (>5 lobes), pancytopenia** with **ineffective erythropoiesis** — raised **LDH and indirect bilirubin**, low reticulocytes.
- **Subacute combined degeneration** — dorsal columns and corticospinal tracts: loss of vibration and proprioception, brisk knee jerks with absent ankle jerks and extensor plantars; peripheral neuropathy; dementia; optic atrophy. **Neurology can occur without anaemia.**
- Causes: **pernicious anaemia** (anti-intrinsic factor and anti-parietal cell antibodies, atrophic gastritis, ↑ gastric cancer risk), gastrectomy, ileal resection/Crohn, **fish tapeworm (D. latum)**, blind loop, **strict vegan diet**, **metformin and PPIs**, and **nitrous oxide** (oxidises cobalamin — a classic exam cause of acute SCD in abusers).
- **B6 (pyridoxal phosphate)** is the cofactor for **cystathionine β-synthase and cystathionase** — deficiency also raises homocysteine; **homocystinuria** is CBS deficiency (marfanoid, **downward lens dislocation**, thrombosis, treat with pyridoxine).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Which enzyme depends on the deficient vitamin?” — the two-enzyme list is the whole answer. Folate reductase and the cystathionine enzymes are always the distractors.

**REMEMBER:** *Two enzymes only: methionine synthase (blood) and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase (nerves).*

**IMAGES:**
![question](https://cdn1.dailyrounds.org/uploads/8a5d8832f8fd47d5a510b47e7da82c7d.PNG)

### 145. Small Cell Lung Cancer and SIADH

**PEARL:** A heavy smoker with a **central/hilar mass** and small blue cells on biopsy has **small cell lung carcinoma** → expect **HYPONATRAEMIA from SIADH**. **Hypercalcaemia belongs to SQUAMOUS** cell carcinoma (PTH-related peptide).

**UNDERSTAND:** Small cell carcinoma arises from **neuroendocrine (Kulchitsky) cells**, so it is intrinsically capable of **synthesising and secreting peptide hormones** — ADH and ACTH being the commonest. Squamous carcinoma has no such machinery; its hypercalcaemia comes from **PTHrP**, a mimic of a paracrine growth factor, and from bone metastases. The histological origin predicts the syndrome.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **SCLC paraneoplastic syndromes: SIADH (hyponatraemia), ectopic ACTH (Cushing — hypokalaemic alkalosis, hyperpigmentation, rapid onset without the classic body habitus), LAMBERT–EATON myasthenic syndrome (proximal weakness that IMPROVES with repeated use; anti-VGCC antibodies), paraneoplastic cerebellar degeneration and limbic encephalitis (anti-Hu).**
- **Squamous cell:** **hypercalcaemia (PTHrP)**, central cavitating lesion, keratin pearls and intercellular bridges. **Adenocarcinoma:** peripheral, commonest overall and in **non-smokers and women**, **hypertrophic pulmonary osteoarthropathy**, **EGFR/ALK/ROS1** targetable mutations. **Large cell:** peripheral, undifferentiated, gynaecomastia.
- **SCLC is staged as limited (confined to one hemithorax, treatable in a single radiation field) vs extensive** and is **NOT a surgical disease** — treat with **platinum–etoposide chemotherapy + radiotherapy** (+ immunotherapy in extensive stage); it is exquisitely chemosensitive but relapses early. Prophylactic cranial irradiation for responders.
- **SIADH:** hyponatraemia with **low serum osmolality, inappropriately concentrated urine (>100 mOsm/kg), urine sodium >40 mmol/L, euvolaemia, normal thyroid/adrenal function**. Treat with **fluid restriction**, salt/urea, **tolvaptan**; correct **no faster than 8–10 mmol/L per 24 h** to avoid **osmotic demyelination (central pontine myelinolysis)**.
- **Pancoast tumour** — apical, causes **Horner syndrome and lower brachial plexus (C8–T1) pain**; **SVC obstruction** is most often caused by SCLC.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Match the histology to the electrolyte. **Small cell → low sodium; squamous → high calcium.**

**REMEMBER:** *Neuroendocrine cells secrete hormones — small cell drops the sodium.*

**IMAGES:**
![question](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2023/04/15/untitled-443.jpg)

---

### 146. Iron Studies

**PEARL:** In **iron deficiency anaemia: serum iron LOW, TIBC/transferrin HIGH, ferritin LOW, and TRANSFERRIN SATURATION LOW (<15%)**. In **anaemia of chronic disease: iron low, TIBC LOW, ferritin HIGH or normal.** In **haemochromatosis/haemosiderosis: iron high, ferritin high, SATURATION HIGH (>45–50%)**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Transferrin behaves as a **negative feedback carrier**: when iron stores are empty the liver makes **more transferrin** to scavenge every available atom, so TIBC rises while the iron available to fill it falls — hence a **doubly depressed saturation (iron/TIBC)**. In inflammation, **hepcidin** rises, locking iron inside macrophages and enterocytes: serum iron falls, but stores are full (**high ferritin**) and transferrin, being a **negative acute-phase reactant**, falls too. One hormone and one acute-phase logic explain both patterns.

**MUST KNOW:**

| | Serum iron | **TIBC/transferrin** | **Ferritin** | **Saturation** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Iron deficiency** | ↓ | **↑** | **↓↓** | **↓ (<15%)** |
| **Anaemia of chronic disease** | ↓ | **↓** | **↑/normal** | Normal or ↓ |
| **Haemochromatosis / iron overload** | ↑ | ↓ | **↑↑** | **↑ (>45%)** |
| **Thalassaemia trait** | Normal/↑ | Normal | Normal/↑ | Normal |
| **Sideroblastic anaemia** | ↑ | Normal/↓ | ↑ | ↑ |

- **Ferritin is the single best test for iron deficiency** (<15 ng/mL diagnostic; <30 highly suggestive) — but it is an **acute-phase reactant**, so a “normal” ferritin does not exclude deficiency in inflammation; use **soluble transferrin receptor** (raised in deficiency, normal in ACD) or a **ferritin threshold of <100** in that setting.
- **Iron deficiency in an adult male or postmenopausal woman = GI blood loss until proven otherwise** → endoscopy and colonoscopy; in India, also consider **hookworm** and coeliac disease.
- **Hereditary haemochromatosis: HFE C282Y**; triad of **cirrhosis, diabetes (“bronze diabetes”) and skin pigmentation**, plus arthropathy (2nd/3rd MCP joints), cardiomyopathy and hypogonadism. **Treat with venesection** (chelation with deferasirox/deferoxamine when anaemic, as in thalassaemia).
- **Haemosiderosis** = iron deposition **without tissue damage** (e.g. from repeated transfusion); haemochromatosis implies organ injury.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Pick the one condition where the **saturation falls** — only true iron deficiency depresses iron **and** raises TIBC.

**REMEMBER:** *Empty stores raise the carrier and drop the saturation; inflammation drops both.*

---

### 147. Fostamatinib in Immune Thrombocytopenia

**PEARL:** **Fostamatinib is a SPLEEN TYROSINE KINASE (SYK) inhibitor** used in **chronic immune thrombocytopenia**. It works by **blocking Fc-receptor signalling in macrophages**, so antibody-coated platelets are no longer destroyed. **Eltrombopag and romiplostim are thrombopoietin receptor agonists, not tyrosine kinase inhibitors.**

**UNDERSTAND: **ITP has two therapeutic angles:** stop the destruction** or **increase the production**. Fostamatinib takes the first route — SYK is the intracellular signal downstream of the **FcγR** on splenic macrophages, so inhibiting it is a **pharmacological splenectomy**. TPO agonists take the second route, stimulating megakaryocytes to outpace the destruction. Knowing which lever a drug pulls answers every ITP drug question.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **ITP** — autoantibodies (usually **anti-GPIIb/IIIa or GPIb/IX**) against platelets; **isolated thrombocytopenia with an otherwise normal blood count and film**; diagnosis of **exclusion**. Bone marrow shows **normal or increased megakaryocytes**.
- **Treatment ladder:** observe if platelets **>30 × 10⁹/L and no bleeding** → **corticosteroids (prednisolone or dexamethasone pulses)** first-line, **IVIG** when a rapid rise is needed (bleeding, surgery, pregnancy) → second-line **rituximab, TPO agonists (eltrombopag, romiplostim, avatrombopag), fostamatinib, splenectomy**.
- **Platelet transfusion is reserved for life-threatening bleeding** — transfused platelets are destroyed just as fast.
- **Secondary ITP:** **SLE, antiphospholipid syndrome, HIV, hepatitis C, H. pylori, CLL, drugs** — always test for **HIV and HCV**.
- **Childhood ITP** typically follows a viral illness, is acute and **self-limiting (>80% resolve spontaneously)**; adult ITP is usually chronic.
- **Do not confuse with TTP** — pentad of thrombocytopenia, **microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (schistocytes)**, fever, renal impairment and neurological signs, from **ADAMTS13 deficiency** → treat with **plasma exchange**; **platelet transfusion is contraindicated**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem names the drug **class** (tyrosine kinase inhibitor). Only fostamatinib qualifies; bosutinib is a BCR-ABL inhibitor for CML.

**REMEMBER:** *SYK inhibition switches off the macrophage — a chemical splenectomy.*

---

### 148. Sickle Cell Fragility

**PEARL:** Sickle cells have **HIGH mechanical fragility** (rigid, non-deformable → they shear and haemolyse) but **LOW osmotic fragility** (they are relatively dehydrated with a high surface-area-to-volume ratio, so they tolerate swelling in hypotonic solution). Thalassaemic cells behave the same way.

**UNDERSTAND:** Osmotic fragility measures **how much water a cell can take up before it bursts** — it is really a test of **surface area relative to volume**. A **spherocyte** has lost membrane, is already spherical, and bursts at almost normal tonicity → **high osmotic fragility**. A **target cell** (thalassaemia, sickle cell, iron deficiency, liver disease) has **excess membrane for its haemoglobin content**, so it can swell a long way → **low osmotic fragility**. Mechanical fragility is a different property — it measures **deformability**, and sickled or rigid cells fail that test badly.

**MUST KNOW:**

| Condition | Mechanical fragility | Osmotic fragility |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Hereditary spherocytosis** | High | **HIGH (the classic)** |
| **Sickle cell disease** | **High** | **LOW** |
| **Thalassaemia** | High | **LOW** |
| **Iron deficiency (target cells)** | — | Low |
| **Malaria** | High | High |

- **Sickling** occurs when deoxygenated **HbS polymerises**; triggers are **hypoxia, dehydration, acidosis, infection, cold and stress**. Repeated sickling makes the membrane permanently rigid (irreversibly sickled cells) → **vaso-occlusion and extravascular haemolysis**.
- **Sickle cell trait is protective against falciparum malaria** — the reason for its geographic distribution (balanced polymorphism).
- Complications: **vaso-occlusive crisis, acute chest syndrome (the leading cause of death), splenic sequestration, autosplenectomy → encapsulated organism sepsis, aplastic crisis with PARVOVIRUS B19, avascular necrosis of the femoral head, priapism, stroke, renal papillary necrosis, leg ulcers, pigment gallstones**.
- Management: **hydroxyurea** (raises HbF), hydration, analgesia, oxygen, **penicillin prophylaxis and vaccination**, transfusion/exchange for stroke and acute chest syndrome, **transcranial Doppler screening in children**; **haematopoietic stem cell transplant and gene therapy** are curative options.
- **Osmotic fragility testing** is now largely replaced by **EMA binding and flow cytometry** for spherocytosis.

**EXAM CONNECTION: **Straight two-variable matching. Remember the rule:** cells with too much membrane (targets) resist osmotic lysis; cells with too little (spherocytes) do not.**

**REMEMBER:** *Sickle cells break under force but survive water — mechanically fragile, osmotically tough.*

---

### 149. Differentiation Syndrome in Acute Promyelocytic Leukaemia

**PEARL:** **Fever, dyspnoea, pulmonary infiltrates, weight gain, oedema, hypotension and renal impairment days after starting ATRA (all-trans retinoic acid) or arsenic trioxide for APML = DIFFERENTIATION SYNDROME** — treat immediately with **high-dose DEXAMETHASONE (10 mg twice daily)**.

**UNDERSTAND:** ATRA does not kill the leukaemic promyelocytes; it **forces them to differentiate into mature granulocytes**. Those newly mature cells **express adhesion molecules and release inflammatory cytokines**, so they marginate in the pulmonary microvasculature and cause a **capillary leak syndrome**. The syndrome is thus a consequence of the treatment working — which is why the answer is a **corticosteroid to suppress the cytokine response**, not more chemotherapy, and why treatment must not be delayed for confirmation.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **APML = AML-M3, t(15;17) PML–RARA fusion**; blasts contain **abundant AUER RODS (“faggot cells”)**. It is the AML subtype that classically presents with **life-threatening DIC** — fibrinogen and platelets must be replaced aggressively.
- **ATRA is started on clinical SUSPICION, before cytogenetic confirmation**, because it rapidly reverses the coagulopathy — the leading early cause of death.
- **APML is now the most curable form of AML** — the combination of **ATRA + ARSENIC TRIOXIDE** achieves cure in most patients **without conventional chemotherapy**.
- **Differentiation syndrome occurs in ~25%, typically in the first 1–3 weeks**; risk is highest with a **rapidly rising white cell count** — cytoreduction (hydroxyurea) may be added. **Steroid prophylaxis** is used in high-risk patients.
- **Arsenic trioxide also causes QT PROLONGATION** — monitor the ECG and electrolytes.
- Distinguish from **tumour lysis syndrome** (hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hyperuricaemia, hypocalcaemia and AKI — prevent with hydration and **rasburicase/allopurinol**) and from **neutropenic sepsis or pneumonia**, which the syndrome closely mimics — in practice antibiotics are given alongside the steroid.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem gives a **recently treated APML patient with new respiratory symptoms and infiltrates**. The answer is the steroid, not another cytotoxic drug.

**REMEMBER:** *The drug works, the cells mature, the lungs leak — give dexamethasone.*

---

### 150. Polycythaemia: Check the Erythropoietin First

**PEARL:** In any patient with a raised haemoglobin, the discriminating test is the **SERUM ERYTHROPOIETIN level**: **LOW EPO = POLYCYTHAEMIA VERA (primary, autonomous — then test JAK2)**; **HIGH or normal EPO = SECONDARY polycythaemia (hypoxia — high altitude, COPD, sleep apnoea, cyanotic heart disease, smoking — or an EPO-secreting tumour)**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Erythropoietin is the **physiological feedback signal**. In a **primary** marrow disorder the red cell mass rises independently of any stimulus, so the normal feedback **suppresses EPO to a low level**. In a **secondary** polycythaemia the raised red cell mass is the *consequence* of a high EPO drive, so EPO is high or inappropriately normal. One test therefore splits the differential in two before any expensive genetic testing.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Polycythaemia vera: JAK2 V617F mutation in >95%**; features include **ERYTHROMELALGIA, aquagenic PRURITUS (itching after a hot bath), splenomegaly, thrombosis (the leading cause of death, arterial and venous — including BUDD–CHIARI syndrome), gout, and a low EPO.** **Treatment: VENESECTION to a haematocrit <45%, LOW-DOSE ASPIRIN, and HYDROXYUREA (or ruxolitinib) for high-risk patients.**
- **Secondary polycythaemia at high altitude** is a physiological adaptation — the patient in the stem also has a **leucoerythroblastic-looking picture with myelocytes**, so **CML must be excluded** (BCR-ABL/Philadelphia chromosome) if the EPO and clinical setting do not fit.
- **RELATIVE (spurious/Gaisböck) polycythaemia:** raised haematocrit from **plasma volume contraction** — dehydration, diuretics, burns; the **red cell mass is normal**.
- **EPO-secreting tumours: RENAL CELL CARCINOMA, hepatocellular carcinoma, cerebellar haemangioblastoma, uterine fibroids, phaeochromocytoma.** Also consider **exogenous EPO or androgen abuse** in athletes.
- **Other myeloproliferative neoplasms: essential thrombocythaemia (JAK2/CALR/MPL), primary myelofibrosis (dry tap, TEARDROP cells, massive splenomegaly, reticulin fibrosis on biopsy — hence the reticulin stain option), and CML (Philadelphia chromosome, basophilia, low LAP score).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Next step” in a polycythaemic patient with an environmental clue (**high altitude**) → **erythropoietin level** before JAK2 or a marrow biopsy.

**REMEMBER:** *Low EPO means the marrow is doing it by itself.*

**IMAGES:**
![explanation](https://media.cerebellumacademy.com/media/public/images/2024/09/05/screenshot-2024-09-02-132158.png)

### 151. TACO vs TRALI

**PEARL:** Respiratory distress after transfusion with **HYPERTENSION and raised BNP = TACO** (circulatory overload) — investigate with **chest X-ray and BNP**. **TRALI causes HYPOtension** and is associated with **anti-leucocyte antibodies**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The two produce identical pictures on the chest film — bilateral infiltrates and hypoxia — because both fill the alveoli. The difference is **hydrostatic vs permeability** oedema. TACO is a **volume problem**: filling pressures rise, so blood pressure and BNP rise. TRALI is **capillary leak** triggered by donor antibodies against recipient neutrophils, so the patient is **vasodilated, hypotensive and often febrile**, with normal filling pressures.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **TACO:** within **6 hours**, hypertension, raised **BNP**, raised JVP, responds to **stopping the transfusion, oxygen and diuretics**. Risk factors: **elderly, cardiac or renal dysfunction, pre-existing fluid overload, large volumes, rapid transfusion**.
- **TRALI:** within **6 hours**, **hypotension, fever, hypoxia**, normal BNP and JVP; **diuretics do NOT help** — treat with **supportive ventilation**. Implicated: **plasma-rich components** from **multiparous female donors** (HLA/HNA antibodies) — hence male-predominant plasma donation policies.
- **TRALI is a leading cause of transfusion-related death**; **TACO is the commonest serious transfusion reaction**.
- Other reactions to keep separate: **acute haemolytic (ABO incompatibility — fever, loin pain, haemoglobinuria)**, **febrile non-haemolytic (leucocyte cytokines — commonest overall)**, **anaphylaxis in IgA deficiency**, **bacterial contamination (platelets, highest risk)**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The word to hunt is the **blood pressure**. Elevated → TACO → **CXR + BNP**. Low → TRALI → leucocyte antibodies.

**REMEMBER:** *Overload pushes pressure up; TRALI drops it.*

---

### 152. Hairy Cell Leukaemia

**PEARL:** **Cladribine (2-CDA) is the drug of choice for hairy cell leukaemia** — a purine analogue with **curative** activity.

**UNDERSTAND:** Hairy cells lack **adenosine deaminase** activity relative to their purine load, so a **deoxyadenosine analogue accumulates** and triggers apoptosis even in these slowly dividing cells. That biochemical vulnerability is why a single short course produces durable complete remissions in a chronic leukaemia.

**MUST KNOW:**

- Presentation: **middle-aged man, massive splenomegaly WITHOUT lymphadenopathy, pancytopenia and monocytopenia**, recurrent infection (**atypical mycobacteria**).
- **DRY TAP on bone marrow aspiration** from marrow fibrosis — biopsy is required.
- Cells: **TRAP-positive**, **CD11c, CD25, CD103, CD123 positive**, with cytoplasmic projections.
- **BRAF V600E mutation** is present in essentially all cases → **vemurafenib** for refractory disease. Second-line/adjunct: **pentostatin (deoxycoformycin)**, **rituximab** (deepens remission), interferon-α, splenectomy.
- Cladribine’s dose-limiting toxicity is **myelosuppression**; it is also used in **CLL, low-grade lymphoma, Langerhans cell histiocytosis and Waldenström’s**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Drug-of-choice recall. Vemurafenib is the tempting modern answer but is reserved for **refractory** disease.

**REMEMBER:** *Cladribine cures the hairy cell.*

---

### 153. Reading BT, PT and aPTT Together

**PEARL:** **Prolonged BT + normal PT + prolonged aPTT = von Willebrand disease.** **Normal BT + normal PT + prolonged aPTT = haemophilia A or B.**

**UNDERSTAND:** vWF has **two jobs**: it mediates **platelet adhesion to subendothelial collagen** (so its loss prolongs the bleeding time) and it **carries and protects factor VIII** in plasma (so its loss also drops factor VIII and prolongs the aPTT). Haemophilia touches only the second function — platelets work normally, so the bleeding time is normal.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **vWD is the commonest inherited bleeding disorder** and is usually **autosomal dominant** — hence affected fathers **and** sisters in the family history, unlike X-linked haemophilia. Presents with **mucocutaneous bleeding: epistaxis, menorrhagia, bleeding after dental extraction**.
- vWF is stored in endothelial **Weibel–Palade bodies** and platelet **α-granules**.
- Treatment: **desmopressin (DDAVP)** for type 1 (releases stored vWF), **vWF-containing factor VIII concentrate** for type 3 and type 2B; **tranexamic acid** as an adjunct.
- **Haemophilia** presents with **deep bleeding — haemarthrosis and muscle haematoma** in a **male child**, after trivial trauma. **A = factor VIII, B = factor IX (Christmas disease)**; both X-linked recessive.
- Test logic: **PT = extrinsic (factor VII)**; **aPTT = intrinsic (VIII, IX, XI, XII)**; **both prolonged = common pathway (II, V, X, fibrinogen)** or liver disease/DIC/warfarin overdose.
- **Mixing study**: corrects → factor deficiency; fails to correct → **inhibitor (e.g. lupus anticoagulant, acquired factor VIII inhibitor)**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Always identify the **bleeding pattern first** (mucosal vs deep), then match the three-test grid. Menorrhagia with a dominant family history is vWD; haemarthrosis in a boy is haemophilia.

**REMEMBER:** *vWF does two jobs, so it breaks two tests.*

**IMAGES:**
![question](https://cdn1.dailyrounds.org/uploads/6359e01906c647fc90ca09a466f82172.PNG)
![explanation](https://dhmbxeygs57ff.cloudfront.net/uploads/0fedd76b578148fc8ed55da37e66574cx1280x1995.JPEG)

### 154. Corrected Reticulocyte Count

**PEARL:** **CRC = Reticulocyte % × (Actual Hb / Normal Hb)** — or equivalently **× (Actual PCV / Normal PCV)**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- Normal Hb taken as 15 g/dL; normal PCV as 45%.
- **CRC >2–3% = adequate marrow response** → haemolysis or blood loss. **CRC <2% = hypoproliferative** marrow → deficiency, aplasia, marrow infiltration, CKD.
- **RETICULOCYTE PRODUCTION INDEX (RPI) = CRC ÷ maturation factor**, applied when **polychromatophilic macrocytes (“shift cells”) appear on the smear** — prematurely released reticulocytes survive longer in blood and inflate the count. **Maturation factor: Hct 45% → 1.0; 35% → 1.5; 25% → 2.0; 15% → 2.5.**
- **Worked example: 9% reticulocytes at Hb 5 g/dL is really 9 × 5/15 = 3%** — an apparently brisk response that is barely adequate.

**REMEMBER:** *Correct the retic for the anaemia; >2% means the marrow is working.*

---

### 155. Desmopressin in Haemophilia A

**PEARL:** **Intranasal (or IV) desmopressin raises factor VIII levels** — effective for **mild to moderate** bleeding in haemophilia A.

**UNDERSTAND:** DDAVP releases stored **factor VIII and vWF from Weibel–Palade bodies of endothelium** — it works only if there is a store to release, so it is **useless in severe haemophilia A** (and in **haemophilia B**, where factor IX is not stored this way).

**MUST KNOW:** Also used in **von Willebrand disease type 1** (contraindicated in **type 2B** — causes thrombocytopenia). Side effects: **hyponatraemia/water retention** and tachyphylaxis with repeated doses. Severe haemophilia A needs **factor VIII concentrate** (or emicizumab).

**REMEMBER:** *DDAVP empties the endothelial store — no store, no effect.*

---

### 156. Hydroxyurea in Beta-Thalassaemia Intermedia

**PEARL:** Hydroxyurea **induces HbF synthesis**, reducing anaemia, decreasing ineffective erythropoiesis, and improving oxygen-carrying capacity in **β-thalassaemia intermedia**.

**UNDERSTAND:** HbF (α2γ2) needs no β chains — so raising it directly compensates for absent β-globin and **rebalances the α:β chain ratio**, which is what actually kills erythroid precursors.

**MUST KNOW:** Same drug, same mechanism, is the mainstay in **sickle cell disease**. It reduces (but does not abolish) transfusion requirements; monitor for **myelosuppression**.

**REMEMBER:** *Hydroxyurea turns the gamma gene back on — helps both thalassaemia intermedia and sickle cell.*

---

### 157. Platelet Storage

**PEARL:** Platelets are stored **in plasma for 5 days at ~22°C (20–24°C) with continuous gentle agitation**.

**UNDERSTAND:** They are kept at room temperature because refrigeration causes clustering and rapid clearance — which is exactly why the shelf life is short: **room temperature allows bacterial growth**, and **bacterial contamination is the leading infectious risk of platelet transfusion**.

**MUST KNOW: **Contrast:** packed red cells — 1–6°C for 35–42 days**; **fresh frozen plasma — −30°C for 1 year**; **cryoprecipitate — −30°C for 1 year**.

**REMEMBER:** *Platelets: warm, shaken, and only 5 days.*

---

### 158. MDS with Isolated del(5q)

**PEARL:** A distinct subtype of myelodysplastic syndrome with a **relatively good prognosis**; **treatment of choice = LENALIDOMIDE**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- Classic patient: an **elderly woman** with **macrocytic anaemia, a normal or HIGH platelet count**, and **hypolobated (mononuclear) megakaryocytes** — the “**5q− syndrome**”.
- Lenalidomide produces **transfusion independence** in ~two-thirds and can cause **cytogenetic remission**.
- Low risk of transformation to AML; the **IPSS-R** score guides overall MDS management (azacitidine/decitabine for high risk, allogeneic HSCT for the fit young).

**REMEMBER:** *5q− = old lady, big red cells, high platelets, funny megakaryocytes → lenalidomide.*

---

### 159. Targeted Agents — Quick Grid

**PEARL:**

- **Vemurafenib** → **BRAF inhibitor** (BRAF V600E **melanoma**)
- **Alemtuzumab** → **anti-CD52** monoclonal antibody
- **Lonafarnib** → **farnesyl transferase inhibitor**
- **Vorinostat** → **HDAC (histone deacetylase) inhibitor** — used in **cutaneous T-cell lymphoma**, including **mycosis fungoides and Sézary syndrome**

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Alemtuzumab** (CD52 is on both B and T lymphocytes) is used in **CLL and in multiple sclerosis**, causing profound, prolonged lymphopenia → **opportunistic infection (CMV reactivation)**.
- **Lonafarnib** is approved for **Hutchinson–Gilford progeria** — it blocks farnesylation of the abnormal **progerin**.
- **Vemurafenib must not be used in BRAF wild-type tumours** (paradoxical MAPK activation); it causes **cutaneous squamous cell carcinomas/keratoacanthomas** — reduced by adding a MEK inhibitor.
- Other CTCL options: bexarotene, romidepsin (also an HDAC inhibitor), PUVA, mogamulizumab.

**REMEMBER:** *Vemurafenib–BRAF, Alemtuzumab–CD52, Lonafarnib–farnesyl (progeria), Vorinostat–HDAC (CTCL).*

---

### 160. Emperipolesis

**PEARL:** **Intact lymphocytes or other cells lying WITHIN the cytoplasm of a histiocyte/plasma cell — without being destroyed.** Classically seen in:

- **Rosai–Dorfman disease** — the **hallmark** feature
- **Autoimmune hepatitis** — plasma cells with emperipolesis
- **Hodgkin lymphoma** — occasionally in Reed–Sternberg cells

**UNDERSTAND:** The distinction from **phagocytosis** is the whole point — in emperipolesis the engulfed cell is **alive, intact and can walk out again**; in phagocytosis it is digested.

**MUST KNOW:** Rosai–Dorfman disease = **sinus histiocytosis with massive lymphadenopathy** — painless bilateral cervical nodes in a young person, with **S100-positive, CD1a-NEGATIVE** histiocytes (contrast **Langerhans cell histiocytosis: S100+ AND CD1a+/langerin+**).

**REMEMBER:** *Cells alive inside a histiocyte = emperipolesis = Rosai–Dorfman (S100+, CD1a−).*

---

### 161. B-cell Acute Lymphoblastic Leukaemia — Markers

**PEARL:** Blasts expressing **CD19, CD20 and CD79a** are of **B-cell lineage** → **B-ALL**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **B-lineage:** CD19, CD20, CD79a, CD22, **CD10 (CALLA — good prognosis)**, TdT.
- **T-lineage:** CD3 (most specific), CD2, CD5, CD7 — T-ALL classically presents in an **adolescent boy with a mediastinal mass**.
- **TdT is positive in both** and marks a **lymphoblast** (absent in AML); **myeloperoxidase and Auer rods** indicate AML.
- Prognosis in B-ALL: **hyperdiploidy and t(12;21) are good; t(9;22) (Philadelphia) and hypodiploidy are poor**.

**REMEMBER:** *CD19/20/79a = B; CD3 = T; TdT = lymphoblast either way.*

---

### 162. Pseudo-Gaucher Cells

**PEARL:** A **large macrophage engorged with ingested haematopoietic cells** (a **pseudo-Gaucher / sea-blue histiocyte-like** appearance) is classically associated with **high cell turnover states — especially chronic myeloid leukaemia**.

**UNDERSTAND:** These macrophages are **not** enzyme-deficient — they are simply **overwhelmed by the volume of cell membrane (glucocerebroside) they must digest**. True Gaucher disease is a **glucocerebrosidase deficiency**.

**MUST KNOW:** Also seen in **thalassaemia, sickle cell disease, and other myeloproliferative disorders**. True Gaucher cells have a “**crumpled tissue paper**” cytoplasm; sea-blue histiocytes stain blue with Giemsa/Wright.

**REMEMBER:** *Too much turnover, not too little enzyme = pseudo-Gaucher (think CML).*

## Infectious Diseases

### 163. Radical Cure in Malaria

**PEARL:** **Artemisinins, chloroquine and sulfadoxine–pyrimethamine achieve CLINICAL cure by killing the ERYTHROCYTIC (asexual blood) stages.** **PRIMAQUINE does NOT produce clinical cure** — it is the **only drug that eradicates the LIVER HYPNOZOITES of *P. vivax* and *P. ovale*, giving RADICAL cure and preventing relapse.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The distinction the question tests is **clinical cure (relief of the acute attack — a blood-stage action) versus radical cure (preventing relapse — a liver-stage action)**. Primaquine has almost no effect on the blood stages, so it cannot treat the acute illness on its own; conversely, the blood schizonticides cannot touch dormant hypnozoites.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Only *P. vivax* and *P. ovale* form HYPNOZOITES and relapse** — *P. falciparum* and *P. malariae* do not (falciparum “recrudesces” from persisting blood forms instead).
- **CHECK G6PD STATUS BEFORE PRIMAQUINE** — it causes severe haemolysis in deficiency. It is also **contraindicated in pregnancy and infants** (give weekly chloroquine prophylaxis until delivery instead). **Tafenoquine** is the single-dose alternative, with the same G6PD caveat.
- **National (NVBDCP) regimens: *P. vivax* — chloroquine 3 days + PRIMAQUINE 0.25 mg/kg for 14 DAYS. *P. falciparum* — ACT (artesunate + sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine in most of India; artemether–lumefantrine in the North-East) + a SINGLE dose of primaquine on day 2 as a GAMETOCYTOCIDE (to block transmission).**
- **Severe malaria: IV ARTESUNATE is the drug of choice** (superior to quinine — AQUAMAT/SEAQUAMAT trials), followed by a full oral ACT course. Watch for **delayed post-artesunate haemolysis**.
- **ACTs pair a fast-acting, rapidly eliminated artemisinin with a slowly eliminated partner drug** — the artemisinin clears the bulk of parasites quickly and the partner mops up survivors, which both shortens treatment and **protects against resistance**.
- **Chloroquine remains effective for vivax, ovale, malariae and knowlesi**, but not for chloroquine-resistant falciparum.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Complete CLINICAL cure achieved by” → the **blood schizonticides (artemisinin, S-P, chloroquine)** — primaquine is excluded because it gives **radical**, not clinical, cure.

**REMEMBER:** *Blood stages end the fever; only primaquine empties the liver and stops the relapse.*

---

### 164. Suspected Meningitis — The Order of Actions

**PEARL:** **(1) SECURE IV ACCESS AND STABILISE → (2) FUNDOSCOPY (look for papilloedema/raised ICP) → (3) GUARDED LUMBAR PUNCTURE → (4) START CEFTRIAXONE.** The purpose of the sequence is to **avoid CONING (uncal/tonsillar herniation) by never doing an LP in the presence of raised ICP**.

**UNDERSTAND:** In practice the overriding rule is that **ANTIBIOTICS MUST NEVER BE DELAYED for imaging or LP** — blood cultures are taken and the antibiotic given, because CSF sterilises slowly enough that the diagnosis is not lost. When the exam presents a stable patient with no focal signs, the intended sequence is as above; when the patient is sick or CT is needed, **antibiotics come first**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Indications for CT BEFORE LP: focal neurological deficit, new-onset seizure, papilloedema, GCS <10 or altered consciousness, immunocompromise, or a history of CNS disease.** **In all of these, GIVE ANTIBIOTICS FIRST.**
- **Empirical therapy: CEFTRIAXONE (± VANCOMYCIN where pneumococcal resistance is a concern) + AMPICILLIN if >50 years, pregnant, or immunocompromised (to cover *LISTERIA*, which cephalosporins miss).**
- **DEXAMETHASONE 0.15 mg/kg given BEFORE or WITH the first antibiotic dose** reduces mortality and hearing loss in **pneumococcal meningitis in adults and *H. influenzae* in children**. Giving it after the antibiotic is ineffective.
- **CSF PATTERNS — the single most examined table:** **BACTERIAL — turbid, high opening pressure, thousands of cells with NEUTROPHILS, VERY LOW glucose, high protein.** **VIRAL — clear, lymphocytes, NORMAL glucose, mildly raised protein.** **TUBERCULOUS — clear with a FIBRIN WEB (cobweb) on standing, LYMPHOCYTES, LOW glucose, VERY HIGH protein, subacute over weeks.** **FUNGAL (cryptococcal) — lymphocytes, low glucose, high opening pressure, India ink/CrAg positive.**
- **“Subacute onset + lymphocytes + LOW glucose + very high protein” = TUBERCULOUS meningitis** — the low glucose is what separates it from viral. Basal exudates cause **cranial nerve palsies (especially VI) and hydrocephalus**; treat with **ATT plus steroids**.
- **Kernig and Brudzinski signs are specific but INSENSITIVE** — their absence does not exclude meningitis.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Sequencing question → **stabilise, fundoscopy, LP, then antibiotics** (in the stable patient).

**REMEMBER:** *Look at the discs before you put in the needle — but never let the needle delay the antibiotic in a sick patient.*

---

### 165. Meningococcal Chemoprophylaxis

**PEARL:** **RIFAMPICIN is the drug of choice for chemoprophylaxis of close contacts** of meningococcal disease; **ciprofloxacin (single dose) and ceftriaxone (single IM dose, preferred in PREGNANCY) are the alternatives.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Prophylaxis works by **eradicating NASOPHARYNGEAL CARRIAGE**, not by treating disease. That is why the drug must **penetrate respiratory secretions** — penicillin, though it treats meningococcal meningitis effectively, **does NOT reliably clear the carrier state**, and so is useless for prophylaxis. It is also why the **index case must receive prophylaxis before discharge** unless treated with ceftriaxone.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Who gets it: household contacts, those sharing a dormitory/barracks, intimate (kissing) contacts, and healthcare workers with DIRECT exposure to respiratory secretions (mouth-to-mouth, intubation, suctioning WITHOUT a mask)** — not the whole ward or casual contacts.
- **Give it as soon as possible, ideally within 24 hours**; it is of little value after 14 days.
- **Doses: rifampicin 600 mg 12-hourly for 2 days (adults); ciprofloxacin 500 mg single dose; ceftriaxone 250 mg IM single dose.** Warn about **orange discoloration of urine, tears and contact lenses, and the reduced efficacy of oral contraceptives** with rifampicin.
- **Recognise the disease: fever, headache, neck stiffness and a NON-BLANCHING PETECHIAL/PURPURIC RASH.** **Purpura fulminans and WATERHOUSE–FRIDERICHSEN SYNDROME (bilateral adrenal haemorrhage with shock)** are the fulminant forms.
- **Give the first dose of antibiotic (benzylpenicillin or ceftriaxone) IMMEDIATELY in the community** when meningococcal disease is suspected with a petechial rash — do not wait for hospital transfer.
- **Vaccination (conjugate ACWY, MenB) is used for outbreak control, the Hajj, and complement-deficient or asplenic patients.**
- **Recurrent Neisserial infection → check TERMINAL COMPLEMENT (C5–C9).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Drug of choice for chemoprophylaxis” → **rifampicin**.

**REMEMBER:** *Prophylaxis clears the throat, not the meninges — which is why it is rifampicin, not penicillin.*

---

### 166. Pneumonia in HIV — Read the CD4 and the Cough

**PEARL:** **STREPTOCOCCUS PNEUMONIAE remains the commonest cause of bacterial pneumonia in HIV, at ANY CD4 count** — a **PRODUCTIVE cough with LOBAR CONSOLIDATION** is bacterial. ***PNEUMOCYSTIS JIROVECII* causes a DRY cough with DIFFUSE INTERSTITIAL infiltrates and a CD4 <200.**

**UNDERSTAND: **The exam always supplies two discriminators:** the character of the cough and the radiographic pattern**. **Consolidation = alveolar = bacterial**; **interstitial/ground-glass = PCP**. A low CD4 count tells you the patient is *at risk* of opportunistic infection, but it does not override the clinical and radiological pattern.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **HIV patients have an increased risk of pneumococcal disease at all CD4 counts** (from impaired B-cell function and opsonisation) — hence **pneumococcal vaccination** is indicated.
- **PCP: DRY cough, exertional DYSPNOEA and DESATURATION ON EXERTION, a normal chest examination, RAISED LDH, and diffuse bilateral perihilar ground-glass change with a normal CXR in up to 25%.** **Diagnosis by induced sputum/BAL with silver or immunofluorescent staining, or PCR.** ==**Treatment: high-dose CO-TRIMOXAZOLE; ADD STEROIDS if PaO2 <70 mmHg or A-a gradient >35** — the steroid indication is a guaranteed exam point. **Prophylaxis with co-trimoxazole when CD4 <200.**==
- **CD4 thresholds worth owning: <200 — PCP; <100 — TOXOPLASMOSIS and cryptococcus; <50 — CMV retinitis and MAC.**
- **TUBERCULOSIS occurs at ANY CD4 count** and is the commonest opportunistic infection in India; **at low CD4 counts it presents ATYPICALLY — lower lobe or miliary disease, no cavitation, and a NEGATIVE sputum smear** (hence CBNAAT/GeneXpert).
- **Disseminated HISTOPLASMOSIS** — a key Indian differential in advanced HIV: **fever, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, pancytopenia, ORAL/mucosal ULCERS, ADRENAL INSUFFICIENCY**, and **UMBILICATED PAPULES that mimic molluscum contagiosum** with **reticulonodular lung infiltrates**. Diagnose by **urine/serum antigen, bone marrow or blood culture**; treat with **liposomal amphotericin B then itraconazole**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most common causative organism” in a **productive** cough with **lobar consolidation**, whatever the CD4 → ***S. pneumoniae***.

**REMEMBER:** *Wet cough and a lobe = pneumococcus; dry cough and a haze = Pneumocystis; molluscum-like papules with fever = histoplasma.*

### 167. Procalcitonin

**PEARL:** Procalcitonin’s established role is to **guide the DURATION of antibiotic therapy (and support stopping antibiotics)** in lower respiratory tract infection and sepsis. It is **NOT accurate enough to diagnose pneumonia, to choose empirical therapy, or to distinguish fungal from bacterial infection**, and must never be used to **withhold** initial antibiotics in a seriously ill patient.

**UNDERSTAND:** Procalcitonin rises in **bacterial infection (driven by IL-1β, TNF-α and IL-6) and is SUPPRESSED by interferon-γ, which is released in VIRAL infection** — which is why it discriminates bacterial from viral better than CRP. But its **sensitivity is inadequate for a rule-out decision**, so it earns its place in **antibiotic stewardship (de-escalation), not diagnosis**.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Kinetics make it useful: procalcitonin rises within 4–6 hours, peaks at 12–24 hours, and has a half-life of ~24 hours — so it FALLS RAPIDLY when infection is controlled.** CRP is much slower on both ends.
- **A PCT-guided algorithm safely reduces antibiotic duration and exposure without increasing mortality** — the main evidence base.
- **False positives (non-infectious PCT rise): major surgery, trauma, burns, cardiogenic shock, pancreatitis, heat stroke, prolonged severe organ perfusion failure, medullary thyroid carcinoma and small cell lung cancer, and the first days of life.**
- **Ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) is defined as pneumonia occurring >48 hours after intubation** — diagnosis rests on **new/progressive infiltrate PLUS at least two of fever, leucocytosis and purulent secretions**, with **quantitative culture of endotracheal aspirate or BAL**. Neither PCT nor CRP diagnoses it.
- **VAP prevention bundle: head-up 30–45°, daily sedation interruption and assessment for extubation, oral chlorhexidine, subglottic secretion drainage, and DVT/peptic ulcer prophylaxis.**
- **Clinical judgement, imaging and cultures remain primary — biomarkers are adjuncts.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Role of procalcitonin” → **to decide the duration of antibiotics**.

**REMEMBER:** *Procalcitonin tells you when to STOP the antibiotic, not whether to start it.*

---

### 168. Empirical Therapy of Bacterial Meningitis

**PEARL:** Empirical therapy for suspected community-acquired bacterial meningitis is **THIRD-GENERATION CEPHALOSPORIN (CEFTRIAXONE or cefotaxime) + VANCOMYCIN + DEXAMETHASONE**, with **AMPICILLIN added at the extremes of age or in immunosuppression** (for *Listeria*). **Penicillin G remains the drug of choice for CONFIRMED penicillin-SUSCEPTIBLE meningococcus**, but is inadequate empirically.

**UNDERSTAND:** Vancomycin is added not because it is better, but because **penicillin- and cephalosporin-resistant *S. pneumoniae* now exists** — and pneumococcus is the commonest and deadliest cause. You cannot afford to be wrong for the 12–48 hours until sensitivities return, so you cover the resistant organism until you can safely narrow.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **DEXAMETHASONE must be given BEFORE OR WITH the first dose of antibiotics** — it reduces the inflammatory burst caused by bacterial lysis and **reduces mortality and hearing loss, particularly in pneumococcal meningitis and in *H. influenzae* in children.** Given late, it does nothing.
- **NEVER DELAY ANTIBIOTICS FOR A CT OR AN LP.** CT before LP is indicated for **immunocompromise, a history of CNS disease, new-onset seizure, papilloedema, altered consciousness or a focal neurological deficit** — take blood cultures and give antibiotics first.
- **Age-specific pathogens: NEONATES — group B *Streptococcus*, *E. coli*, *Listeria*. CHILDREN/ADULTS — *S. pneumoniae*, *N. meningitidis*. >50 or IMMUNOCOMPROMISED — add *LISTERIA* (hence ampicillin; cephalosporins do NOT cover it). POST-NEUROSURGERY/SHUNT — staphylococci and Gram-negatives (add ceftazidime/meropenem + vancomycin).**
- **CSF patterns: BACTERIAL — high neutrophils, glucose <40% of blood, high protein, high opening pressure. VIRAL — lymphocytes, NORMAL glucose. TB/FUNGAL — lymphocytes, VERY LOW glucose, VERY HIGH protein, cobweb clot.**
- **Add ACYCLOVIR if HSV encephalitis is a possibility** (temporal lobe signs, seizures, personality change) — the cost of missing it is far higher than the cost of treating it.
- **Meningococcal chemoprophylaxis for close contacts: RIFAMPICIN, CIPROFLOXACIN or CEFTRIAXONE.** Look for **purpuric rash and WATERHOUSE–FRIDERICHSEN syndrome** (adrenal haemorrhage with shock).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Empirical treatment of meningococcal meningitis” → **ceftriaxone** (penicillin G only for proven sensitive strains).

**REMEMBER:** *Ceftriaxone plus vancomycin plus steroid before the first dose — and ampicillin at the extremes of age.*

---

### 169. Relative Bradycardia — Faget’s Sign

**PEARL:** **Faget’s sign = pulse–temperature DISSOCIATION**: the heart rate is lower than the fever predicts. Normally the pulse rises **~15–20 beats/min per 1 °C**. Classic causes: **TYPHOID, Q FEVER, LEPTOSPIROSIS, LEGIONELLA, brucellosis, scrub typhus and other rickettsioses, tularaemia, malaria, dengue, and yellow fever.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Most of these are **INTRACELLULAR organisms causing an atypical or toxin-mediated systemic illness rather than a classic pyogenic bacteraemia** — the febrile response and the sympathetic response become uncoupled, in part through direct effects on the sinoatrial node and cardiac conduction. It is a cheap, bedside pointer in exactly the resource-limited settings where these infections are common.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **TYPHOID: step-ladder fever, relative bradycardia, ROSE SPOTS (2–4 mm blanching macules on the trunk), splenomegaly, LEUCOPENIA with an ANEOSINOPHILIA, and constipation in adults. BLOOD CULTURE is the best test in week 1, STOOL/URINE culture in weeks 2–3, and BONE MARROW CULTURE is the most sensitive at any stage (and remains positive despite prior antibiotics). The Widal test is unreliable.** **Treat with ceftriaxone or azithromycin** (fluoroquinolone resistance is now widespread in South Asia). **Complications in week 3: INTESTINAL PERFORATION and HAEMORRHAGE from ulcerated PEYER’S PATCHES.**
- **LEPTOSPIROSIS: exposure to floodwater or rodent urine; CONJUNCTIVAL SUFFUSION WITHOUT DISCHARGE is nearly pathognomonic; severe form = WEIL’S DISEASE (jaundice, renal failure, haemorrhage). Treat with penicillin or doxycycline; doxycycline for prophylaxis.**
- **NON-INFECTIOUS causes of relative bradycardia: DRUG FEVER, BETA-BLOCKERS, calcium channel blockers, lymphoma, factitious fever, and raised intracranial pressure.**
- **The converse — a pulse HIGHER than the fever warrants — suggests pulmonary embolism, hypovolaemia, thyrotoxicosis, or toxin-mediated disease such as diphtheria and tetanus.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Relative bradycardia occurs in” → typically **all of the above**.

**REMEMBER:** *A high fever with a calm pulse means an intracellular organism — think typhoid first.*

---

### 170. Inhalational Anthrax

**PEARL:** The commonest presentation of **inhalational anthrax (woolsorter’s disease)** is **HAEMORRHAGIC MEDIASTINITIS**, with a **WIDENED MEDIASTINUM on chest X-ray and pleural effusions — NOT a pneumonia.** *Bacillus anthracis* is an aerobic, **Gram-positive, spore-forming, NON-MOTILE, capsulated bacillus** in “bamboo stick” chains.

**UNDERSTAND:** Spores are too small to lodge in airways — they are **carried straight to the alveoli, phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages, and transported to the MEDIASTINAL LYMPH NODES, where they germinate.** The pathology is therefore **lymphatic, not alveolar** — which is exactly why the X-ray shows a widened mediastinum with clear lungs, and why calling it “anthrax pneumonia” is wrong.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **Virulence factors: the POLY-D-GLUTAMIC ACID CAPSULE (anti-phagocytic — the only bacterial capsule made of protein rather than polysaccharide) and the TRIPARTITE TOXIN — PROTECTIVE ANTIGEN (binds and delivers), OEDEMA FACTOR (a calmodulin-dependent ADENYLATE CYCLASE → massive oedema) and LETHAL FACTOR (a zinc metalloprotease cleaving MAP kinase kinase → cell death and shock).** Protective antigen is the vaccine target.
- **CUTANEOUS anthrax (>95% of natural cases) — a painless papule becoming a vesicle then a black ESCHAR (“MALIGNANT PUSTULE”) with striking non-pitting oedema and NO pus** (the toxin is anti-inflammatory); it is painless, which distinguishes it from staphylococcal lesions.
- **GASTROINTESTINAL anthrax** follows eating undercooked infected meat and carries high mortality. **ANTHRAX MENINGITIS is haemorrhagic — the “CARDINAL’S CAP”.**
- **Treatment: IV CIPROFLOXACIN (or doxycycline) PLUS CLINDAMYCIN/linezolid (to suppress toxin synthesis) for ≥14 days, plus ANTITOXIN (raxibacumab, obiltoxaximab) and steroids for meningitis.** **Post-exposure prophylaxis: ciprofloxacin 500 mg BD for 60 days plus vaccine** — the long duration reflects **prolonged spore dormancy in the lymph nodes**.
- **Anthrax is a CATEGORY A BIOTERRORISM agent** because spores are stable, easily aerosolised and highly lethal by inhalation.
- **Laboratory clues: McFADYEAN’S REACTION (polychrome methylene blue showing pink capsule around blue bacilli), the “MEDUSA HEAD” colony, and the STRING OF PEARLS test on penicillin agar.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** “Most common presentation of inhalational anthrax” → **haemorrhagic mediastinitis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Anthrax spores skip the lung and go to the lymph nodes — a wide mediastinum, not a consolidation.*

---

### 171. Tuberculous Meningitis — CSF Findings

**PEARL:** CSF showing a **LYMPHOCYTIC (mononuclear) pleocytosis, HIGH PROTEIN, LOW GLUCOSE and a COBWEB-LIKE CLOT (pellicle) on standing** = **TUBERCULOUS MENINGITIS.** Acid-fast bacilli are best sought **in that pellicle**; **CSF CULTURE remains the gold standard**, and **CBNAAT/Xpert MTB-RIF gives a rapid answer with rifampicin resistance.**

**UNDERSTAND:** TB meningitis is a **thick, gelatinous EXUDATE that settles at the BASE of the brain** — which explains its entire clinical picture. **Basal exudate traps the CRANIAL NERVES (especially CN VI, then III and VII), obstructs CSF flow at the basal cisterns to cause HYDROCEPHALUS, and produces a vasculitis of the perforating vessels of the circle of Willis → INFARCTS in the basal ganglia and internal capsule.** The very high protein content is what allows the fibrin web to form in the tube.

**MUST KNOW:**

- **The CSF table: BACTERIAL — thousands of NEUTROPHILS, very low glucose, high protein. VIRAL/ASEPTIC — lymphocytes, NORMAL glucose, mildly raised protein. TUBERCULOUS — lymphocytes, VERY LOW glucose, VERY HIGH protein (often >100), cobweb clot. FUNGAL (cryptococcal) — lymphocytes, low glucose, positive INDIA INK and CRYPTOCOCCAL ANTIGEN.** **Early TB meningitis may show a NEUTROPHIL predominance** that shifts to lymphocytes — a real diagnostic trap.
- **The presentation is SUBACUTE — a prodrome of weeks with fever, headache, vomiting, apathy and personality change** — quite unlike the hours-to-days course of pyogenic meningitis. **Cranial nerve palsy and hydrocephalus in a subacute meningitis means TB until proven otherwise.**
- **Contrast MRI shows BASAL MENINGEAL ENHANCEMENT, hydrocephalus, TUBERCULOMAS and basal ganglia infarcts.**
- **Treatment: ATT for at least 12 MONTHS (2 months intensive, then 10 months continuation) — note the extended duration — PLUS ADJUNCTIVE CORTICOSTEROIDS (dexamethasone) for 6–8 weeks, which REDUCE MORTALITY.** **ETHAMBUTOL penetrates the CSF poorly and is often replaced by a fluoroquinolone or by streptomycin/linezolid in regimens.**
- **Do NOT delay treatment for microbiological confirmation** — outcome depends almost entirely on the stage at which therapy begins.
- **Hydrocephalus may need a VP shunt or external ventricular drain.** **SIADH is a common complication.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** CSF image with a cobweb clot + lymphocytes + low sugar → **tuberculous meningitis**.

**REMEMBER:** *A cobweb in the tube and cranial nerves at the base — subacute meningitis is tuberculous.*

**IMAGES:**
![question](https://cdn1.dailyrounds.org/uploads/dafb9bf2283b45b59a0a7914f2e03006.JPEG)
